<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01JJNgFp9C6gYA2KgtTcjb8S`</summary>

```text
365	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
366	\label{app:failure-modes}
367	
368	All 73 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. The deterministic strategy never executes \texttt{focus on}, which explains its 91.8\% survival rate (its 16.3\% death rate arises from other causes such as selecting inherently dangerous actions).
369	
370	\begin{table}[h]
371	\centering
372	\small
373	\begin{tabular}{@{}clr@{}}
374	\toprule
375	Cat. & Description & \% \\
376	\midrule
377	A & No task awareness (pure \texttt{focus on} spam) & 21.9 \\
378	B & Task-aware but no action grounding & 69.9 \\
379	D & Genuine attempt with execution failure & 2.7 \\
380	E & Initial score wasted by early errors & 5.5 \\
381	\bottomrule
382	\end{tabular}
383	\caption{Taxonomy of 73 internal-strategy death episodes. Category B dominates: agents correctly parse the task description but cannot translate understanding into valid ScienceWorld action primitives. 78.1\% of dying agents (B+D+E) demonstrate task comprehension; 97.3\% (A+B) fail to ground any task-relevant action.}
384	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
385	\end{table}
386	
387	\paragraph{Representative examples.}
388	\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \texttt{focus on kitchen}, \texttt{focus on door}, \texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \texttt{focus on} call. \emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.
389	
390	\section{Ablation Studies}
391	\label{app:ablations}
392	
393	\paragraph{Capacity scaling.} We scale the implicit router from Qwen3-0.6B to Qwen3-4B and evaluate at both step and episode level (Table~\ref{tab:capacity}).
394	
```

</details>

```json
  "new_string": "\\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \\texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \\texttt{focus on kitchen}, \\texttt{focus on door}, \\texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \\texttt{focus on} call. \\emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \\texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \\texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.\n\n\\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}\nFigure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy. Both episode types are dominated by \\texttt{connect} actions ($87\\%$ in death, $79\\%$ in non-death), but \\texttt{focus on} usage is $3{\\times}$ higher in death episodes ($5.5\\%$ vs.\\ $1.8\\%$). Among fatal \\texttt{focus on} targets, \\texttt{workshop} accounts for 44.6\\%, suggesting a systematic bias toward specific objects rather than random exploration. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \\texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \\texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison} contrasts the internal and deterministic strategies: the deterministic strategy exhibits a qualitatively different action profile with near-zero \\texttt{focus on} usage, confirming that the grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_distribution.pdf}\n\\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}74$) vs.\\ non-death ($n{=}59$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \\texttt{connect}). Right: non-\\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \\texttt{focus on} is $3{\\times}$ more frequent in death episodes, while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}\n\\label{fig:death-action-dist}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}\n\\caption{Step-by-step action sequences for 6 representative death episodes across different task types. The fatal \\texttt{focus on} action (marked with arrow) consistently appears in the final steps after long \\texttt{connect} sequences, illustrating the ``connect loop $\\to$ fatal focus'' pattern.}\n\\label{fig:death-timeline}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\begin{figure}[h]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_comparison.pdf}\n\\caption{Non-\\texttt{connect} action distribution comparison between internal (left) and deterministic (right) strategies. The deterministic strategy shows minimal \\texttt{focus on} usage across both death and non-death episodes, confirming that the action-space grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.}\n\\label{fig:death-comparison}\n\\end{figure}\n\n\\section{Ablation Studies}"
}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Gaa14xvQkfmun5PjE6Mhss`</summary>

```text
9	
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy never executes \texttt{focus on}, achieving 91.8\% survival. This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.
12	
13	\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}
```

</details>

```json
    "newString": "The deterministic strategy never executes \\texttt{focus on}, achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} alone accounts for 44.6\\%, indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy never executes \\texttt{focus on}, achieving 91.8\\% survival. This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",
```

### Tool result

_source line 923 · 2026-05-18T08:46:06.826Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01NGzwMZwrH1W5n5vEmzC3qm`</summary>

```text
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy never executes \texttt{focus on}, achieving 91.8\% survival (Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \texttt{workshop} alone accounts for 44.6\%, indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.
12	
```

</details>

```text
365	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
366	\label{app:failure-modes}
367	
368	All 73 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. The deterministic strategy never executes \texttt{focus on}, which explains its 91.8\% survival rate (its 16.3\% death rate arises from other causes such as selecting inherently dangerous actions).
369
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy never executes \\texttt{focus on}, achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} alone accounts for 44.6\\%, indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",
```

```json
        "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_12/projects/simgate-modular-routing/docs/paper/discussion.tex",
        "content": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} alone accounts for 44.6\\%, indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",
```

```json
    "content": "- update-config: Use this skill to configure the Claude Code harness via settings.json. Automated behaviors (\"from now on when X\", \"each time X\", \"whenever X\", \"before/after X\") require hooks configured in settings.json - the harness executes these, not Claude, so memory/preferences cannot fulfill them. Also use for: permissions (\"allow X\", \"add permission\", \"move permission to\"), env vars (\"set X=Y\"), hook troubleshooting, or any changes to settings.json/settings.local.json files. Examples: \"allow npm commands\", \"add bq permission to global settings\", \"move permission to user settings\", \"set DEBUG=true\", \"when claude stops show X\". For simple settings like theme/model, suggest the /config command.\n- keybindings-help: Use when the user wants to customize keyboard shortcuts, rebind keys, add chord bindings, or modify ~/.claude/keybindings.json. Examples: \"rebind ctrl+s\", \"add a chord shortcut\", \"change the submit key\", \"customize keybindings\".\n- simplify: Review changed code for reuse, quality, and efficiency, then fix any issues found.\n- fewer-permission-prompts: Scan your transcripts for common read-only Bash and MCP tool calls, then add a prioritized allowlist to project .claude/settings.json to reduce permission prompts.\n- loop: Run a prompt or slash command on a recurring interval (e.g. /loop 5m /foo). Omit the interval to let the model self-pace. - When the user wants to set up a recurring task, poll for status, or run something repeatedly on an interval (e.g. \"check the deploy every 5 minutes\", \"keep running /babysit-prs\"). Do NOT invoke for one-off tasks.\n- schedule: Create, update, list, or run scheduled remote agents (routines) that execute on a cron schedule. - When the user wants to schedule a recurring remote agent, set up automated tasks, create a cron job for Claude Code, or manage their scheduled agents/routines. Also use when the user wants a one-time scheduled run (\"run this once at 3pm\", \"remind me to check X tomorrow\").\n- claude-api: Build, debug, and optimize Claude API / Anthropic SDK apps. Apps built with this skill should include prompt caching. Also handles migrating existing Claude API code between Claude model versions (4.5 → 4.6, 4.6 → 4.7, retired-model replacements).\nTRIGGER when: code imports `anthropic`/`@anthropic-ai/sdk`; user asks for the Claude API, Anthropic SDK, or Managed Agents; user adds/modifies/tunes a Claude feature (caching, thinking, compaction, tool use, batch, files, citations, memory) or model (Opus/Sonnet/Haiku) in a file; questions about prompt caching / cache hit rate in an Anthropic SDK project.\nSKIP: file imports `openai`/other-provider SDK, filename like `*-openai.py`/`*-generic.py`, provider-neutral code, general programming/ML.\n- init: Initialize a new CLAUDE.md file with codebase documentation\n- review: Review a pull request\n- security-review: Complete a security review of the pending changes on the current branch",
```

```text
1	\begin{abstract}
2	Modular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \textbf{78.9\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\%), while $10{\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates to ${\sim}$35\%, yet none closes the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).
3	\end{abstract}
4	
```

```text
1	\section{Discussion}
2	\label{sec:discussion}
3	
4	\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}
5	The router diagnostic (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.
6	
7	\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}
8	Three qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\% vs.\ baseline 42.9\%, ${\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\sim}$35\% ($\Delta \approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\sim}$35\%, $\Delta \approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.
9	
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \texttt{focus on} (${\sim}0.5\%$ of actions vs.\ $5.5\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\% survival (Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \texttt{workshop} alone accounts for 44.6\%, indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.
12	
13	\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}
14	The oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\ $-9.47$). This \emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.
15	
16	\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}
17	Stronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\sim}$51\% to ${\sim}$35\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\%$ (vs.\ $90.4\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\%) remains ${\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\delta = +34.3\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\delta = +26.6\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.
18	
19	\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}
20	Our framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.
21	
22	\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}
23	DAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.
24
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Jepqq4f4jLJ6LHuwb5jP88`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Experiments}
2	
3	We analyze 11 of ScienceWorld's 30 task types (245 episodes); the 19 excluded types lack actionable routing signal due to development contamination (5), taxonomic redundancy (7), or agent-floor performance (7). An anti-cherry-picking analysis confirms that death dominance holds in 18 of these 19 types, with a mean $\Delta\text{DR} = +38.2$\,pp exceeding the included tasks' $+28.7$\,pp (Appendix~\ref{app:per-task}).
4	
5	\subsection{Death Avoidance Decomposition}
6	\label{sec:death-decomp}
7	
8	\begin{figure}[t]
9	\centering
10	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/fig1_daf_scatter.pdf}
11	\caption{Per-task scatter: death rate differential (internal $-$ deterministic) vs.\ performance gap ($\Delta_{\text{full}}$). $r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$, $n = 11$ task types.}
12	\label{fig:decomposition}
13	\end{figure}
14	
15	Across 245 episodes, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = +29.60$; excluding death episodes, $\Delta_{\text{nd}} = +2.84$, yielding DAF $= 90.4\%$ (95\% CI: $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$; at the default $P{=}100$). The penalty-independent death-asymmetry label fraction is 78.9\% (75 of 95 non-tie labels). Given observed $\delta = 0.265$ and $P = 100$, the predicted DAF $= \delta P / (\delta P + \Delta_{\text{nd}}) = 26.5 / 29.37 \approx 90.3\%$, closely matching the observed value; DAF is thus a derived quantity once $\delta$, $P$, and $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$ are known, while the 78.9\% label fraction is the penalty-independent empirical finding. Of these 75, 54 (72.0\%) reduce to ties when the death penalty is removed entirely: in those episodes, both modalities score zero without death, so the only reason one strategy outscored the other was avoiding the $-100$ penalty. Death avoidance is not merely the dominant but often the \emph{sole} discriminating signal between modalities. The remaining 21 death-asymmetry labels involve episodes where the surviving strategy also earned positive score, but the death penalty still accounts for the majority of the score gap.
16	
17	\paragraph{Per-task correlation structure.}
18	The internal strategy dies in 42.9\% of episodes versus 16.3\% for deterministic (2.6$\times$), consistent with the internal strategy's reliance on self-generated observations that compound prediction errors across steps. Per-task, death rate differential correlates with $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ ($r = 0.97$, $p < 10^{-6}$; Figure~\ref{fig:decomposition}). Since $P$ is constant across all task types, $\Delta_{\text{full}} = P \cdot \delta + \Delta_{\text{nd}}$ makes $\Delta_{\text{full}}$ a linear function of $\delta$ with residual $\Delta_{\text{nd}}$; when DAF $\rightarrow 1$, this residual vanishes and $r \rightarrow 1$ by construction. The near-perfect correlation is therefore a mathematical consequence of death dominance, not an independent empirical finding. We retain it as an internal consistency check. The correlation drops below significance at $P \leq 25$ (Appendix~\ref{app:penalty}). Of 245 episodes, 86 are \texttt{det\_preferred}, 9 \texttt{internal\_preferred}, and 150 \texttt{no\_preference}.
19	
20	\paragraph{Signal concentration.}
21	The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels.
22	
23	\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}
24	Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\% CI $[81.6\%, 98.8\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, resampling task types) yields a wider $[73.3\%, 100\%]$; with $K < 40$ this should be interpreted cautiously~\citep{cameron-2008}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$ task clusters, $B{=}9{,}999$) confirms significance at the cluster level ($p < 0.001$, Cohen's $d = 3.02$, 95\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$; Appendix~\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Both intervals exclude 50\%, confirming that death avoidance accounts for a majority of routing signal under resampling. Across all 28 death-prone tasks (included and excluded), internal DR exceeds deterministic DR in every case (100\% directional consistency), with pooled $\Delta = +37.3\%$ (95\% CI: $[29.7\%, 44.4\%]$); 15 of 28 are individually significant, with the 13 non-significant cases entirely explained by small sample size ($n{=}5$; Appendix~\ref{app:per-task-ci}). An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}).
25	
26	\subsection{Multi-Model Validation}
27	\label{sec:cross-model}
28	
29	A Llama-3.1-8B-Instruct~\citep{llama3-2024} replication on 7 task types ($N{=}70$) shows the death-avoidance pattern reproduces and intensifies: internal death rate $34.3\%$ vs.\ deterministic $0\%$ (cf.\ Qwen: $42.9\%$ vs.\ $16.3\%$), with zero internal-preferred episodes (24 det-preferred, 46 no-preference). With $N{=}70$ and zero internal-preferred labels, this preliminary pilot demonstrates directional consistency of the death mechanism across model families, not full framework replication, which would require a larger sample and independent environment. Both models are in the 7--8B range; $10{\times}$ scaling results (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) and Reflexion experiments confirm that death dominance persists across capability levels (Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}).
30	
31	\subsection{Agent Improvement Baselines}
32	\label{sec:agent-improvement}
33	
34	\begin{table}[t]
35	\centering
36	\small
37	\begin{tabular}{@{}lrr@{}}
38	\toprule
39	Condition & Death\% & $N$ \\
40	\midrule
41	Single-trial baseline & 42.9 & 245 \\
42	Retry ($3{\times}$) & 40.9 & 220 \\
43	Reflexion ($3{\times}$) & 31.4 & 220 \\
44	Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ scale) & 34.5$^\dagger$ & 200 \\
45	Deterministic baseline & 16.3 & 245 \\
46	\bottomrule
47	\end{tabular}
48	\caption{Internal-strategy death rates under agent improvement methods. $\dagger$\,72B death rate is the aggregate over 200 episodes across 10 death-prone tasks; \texttt{find-non-living-thing} excluded (0\% death at both scales). Reflexion partially reduces death but the gap to deterministic remains large.}
49	\label{tab:agent-improvement}
50	\end{table}
51	
52	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance. Retry gives the agent three attempts per episode without self-correction; Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} adds verbal self-reflection between trials (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\% vs.\ 42.9\% baseline; the 2.0~pp difference is within sampling noise, Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). Reflexion reduces death to 31.4\% by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths (27 of 92), but the gap to deterministic (16.3\%) remains 15.1~pp. Trial~1 death rate under Reflexion (41.8\%) matches the single-trial baseline; the reduction comes from cross-trial self-correction, not from the Reflexion prompt itself. Trial~3 adds minimal rescue beyond Trial~2 (net $+4$). Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\sim}$51\% to ${\sim}$35\% ($\Delta \approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Task-level variation ranges from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix, where 72B performs slightly worse) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). The 31.4\% in Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement} covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%, the figure reported in the Abstract. Recomputing DAF with 72B as the internal strategy ($N{=}220$) yields $90.9\%$ (95\% CI: $[83.9\%, 97.3\%]$), nearly identical to the 7B value ($90.4\%$); the death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\%$ (7B) to $84.3\%$ (72B). On death-prone tasks ($N{=}200$), DAF reaches $99.5\%$ ($[98.0\%, 100.2\%]$) with $95.9\%$ of labels driven by death asymmetry. A matched 10-task comparison confirms scale-independence: 8 of 10 death-prone tasks maintain DAF $= 100\%$ at both 7B and 72B; episode-weighted DAF declines only from $100\%$ to $97.5\%$. No task shifts from death-dominated to performance-dominated at $10{\times}$ scale. Scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.
53	
54	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
55	\label{sec:math-contrast}
56	
57	On MATH Level~4--5~\citep{math-2021} ($N{=}795$ problems, code interpreter as deterministic modality), internal reasoning and code interpretation achieve near-identical accuracy: $73.5\%$ vs.\ $73.0\%$ ($+0.5$~pp, McNemar $p = 0.81$, 95\% CI $[-2.6, +3.6]$~pp). The label distribution is balanced (107 \texttt{int\_preferred} vs.\ 95 \texttt{det\_preferred}, 74.6\% \texttt{no\_preference}), ruling out routing signal ${\geq}3.6$~pp at $\alpha{=}0.05$. Without catastrophic failure, modality routing confers no advantage.
58	
59	\subsection{Cross-Environment Contrasts}
60	\label{sec:cross-env}
61	
62	We evaluate two additional environments lacking catastrophic death penalties. The ``deterministic'' modality differs by environment: an oracle simulator (ScienceWorld), a code interpreter (MATH), or admissible-action access (ALFWorld). These are not equivalent modality switches: each instantiates ``deterministic computation'' through a qualitatively different mechanism. Results across environments should therefore be interpreted as boundary probes of framework applicability rather than direct replications. The framework's diagnostic value lies in identifying different mechanisms (death avoidance in ScienceWorld, null signal in MATH, information asymmetry in ALFWorld) across heterogeneous settings (Figure~\ref{fig:signal-reversal}).
63	
64	\begin{figure}[t]
65	\centering
66	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/signal_reversal.pdf}
67	\caption{Cross-environment label distributions. ScienceWorld and ALFWorld both show deterministic preference, but through different mechanisms: death avoidance (ScienceWorld) vs.\ information asymmetry from admissible-action access (ALFWorld). MATH shows balanced labels, confirming no routing signal without catastrophic failure. ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities per environment (Table~\ref{tab:cross-env}).}
68	\label{fig:signal-reversal}
69	\end{figure}
70	
71	\paragraph{ALFWorld.} ALFWorld~\citep{alfworld-2021} is a text-based household environment (6 task types, 135 games, max 50 steps). The deterministic strategy additionally receives admissible actions, an information asymmetry rather than a computation mode switch. The success rate gap is 27.4\% vs.\ 3.0\%, with 35 deterministic-preferred, 2 internal-preferred, and 98 no-preference labels (96 both-fail). The deterministic advantage stems from the information gap rather than death avoidance, a qualitatively different mechanism that the framework diagnoses without modification.
72	
73	\begin{table}[t]
74	\centering
75	\small
76	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
77	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccccc@{}}
78	\toprule
79	Environment & $N$ & Det\% & Int\% & NoPref\% & Death? \\
80	\midrule
81	ScienceWorld & 245 & 35.1 & 3.7 & 61.2 & Yes ($-100$) \\
82	MATH & 795 & 12.0 & 13.5 & 74.6 & No \\
83	ALFWorld & 135 & 25.9 & 1.5 & 72.6 & No\textsuperscript{$\dagger$} \\
84	\bottomrule
85	\end{tabular}}
86	\caption{Cross-environment label distribution. ``Deterministic'' denotes qualitatively different modalities: an oracle simulator providing ground-truth state (ScienceWorld), a code interpreter executing symbolic computation (MATH), or admissible-action access providing action-space pruning (ALFWorld). Only ScienceWorld satisfies all framework assumptions (paired modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, sparse rewards); MATH and ALFWorld lack catastrophic outcomes and serve as boundary probes. $\dagger$\,ALFWorld's deterministic advantage stems from information asymmetry, not death avoidance.}
87	\label{tab:cross-env}
88	\end{table}
89	
90	\subsection{Baselines}
91	\label{sec:baselines}
92	
93	\begin{table}[t]
94	\centering
95	\small
96	\resizebox{\columnwidth}{!}{%
97	\begin{tabular}{@{}lccc@{}}
98	\toprule
99	Strategy & Score & 95\% CI & Death \% \\
100	\midrule
101	Always-Internal & $-39.07$ & [$-46.40$, $-32.27$] & 42.9 \\
102	Always-Deterministic & $-9.47$ & [$-14.71$, $-4.29$] & 16.3 \\
103	Random & $-22.38$ & [$-28.67$, $-15.75$] & 27.8 \\
104	Oracle & $-6.58$ & [$-11.54$, $-1.70$] & 14.3 \\
105	\midrule
106	Trained Router$^\dagger$ & $-10.70$ & [$-16.33$, $-5.27$] & 17.6 \\
107	\bottomrule
108	\end{tabular}}
109	\caption{Episode-weighted mean scores on ScienceWorld (11 tasks, 245 episodes). $\dagger$\,Single random seed (42) per episode pair; see Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness} for 8-seed robustness analysis.}
110	\label{tab:baselines}
111	\end{table}
112	
113	Always-deterministic ($-9.47$) outperforms always-internal ($-39.07$) by 29.60 points. The oracle improves only to $-6.58$, with 96.3\% of decisions matching always-deterministic, an \emph{oracle degeneracy}: the optimal routing policy closely resembles unconditional deterministic computation.
114	
115	\subsection{Router Training as Diagnostic Probe}
116	\label{sec:router-diagnostic}
117	
118	We probe whether counterfactual labels can train a step-level router, as a diagnostic of label informativeness. The primary bound on performance is the oracle margin: only 2.89 points separate the oracle ($-6.58$) from always-deterministic ($-9.47$), capping routing upside regardless of router quality. A secondary confound is that ${\sim}27.7\%$ of step-level labels are misaligned with their episode-level assignment, further limiting step-level learnability. These two factors are logically independent: a narrow oracle margin limits routing value even with perfectly accurate labels, while mislabeling degrades learning even if the margin were large. In this setting, the oracle margin is the binding constraint: the 2.89-point ceiling means routing can recover at most modest gains regardless of label quality. An implicit router (Qwen3, LoRA fine-tuned; Appendix~\ref{app:router-details}) is trained at three scales (0.6B, 1.7B, 4B) with identical 4-seed configurations. Step-level balanced accuracy is flat across scales ($56.4 \pm 3.5\%$, $58.2 \pm 4.0\%$, $59.2 \pm 3.5\%$; all within seed variance); step-level discrimination is not capacity-sensitive. At episode level, only 4B exceeds the always-deterministic baseline ($59.0 \pm 1.9\%$, $p < 0.005$; Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}), while 0.6B ($50.7 \pm 2.6\%$) and 1.7B ($43.1 \pm 1.0\%$) remain at or below chance. The 1.7B dip is robust across seeds (range $41.7$--$44.4\%$). We hypothesize that the 1.7B model has sufficient capacity to memorize death-correlated patterns in the training set but insufficient capacity to learn a generalizable routing policy, resulting in spurious anti-correlation; future work should examine per-class routing decisions to test this hypothesis. This non-monotonic pattern, combined with universally low internal recall, reinforces that learned routing does not scale predictably with capacity at current data scales. Unlike single-step tool-calling, the multi-step nature of this routing problem (10--50 actions with compounding state uncertainty) creates inherent label noise between step-level decisions and episode-level outcomes.
119	
120	\paragraph{End-to-end deployment.} Deployed in the full ScienceWorld loop ($N{=}245$), the 4B router scores $-12.03$ (95\% CI: $[-17.82, -6.45]$), not exceeding always-deterministic ($-9.47$; overlapping CIs). Paraphrase-based augmentation ($25\%$--$800\%$, synthetic oversampling without new environment interactions) provides no improvement (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}); augmentation inflates the majority class without generating novel internal-preferred patterns (only 9 of 245 base episodes are internal-preferred), so real-data collection may be necessary but the $3.7\%$ base rate makes it expensive. The agent improvement results (\S\ref{sec:agent-improvement}) reinforce this ceiling: even Reflexion-level intervention leaves a 15.1~pp death gap to deterministic. Failure mode, augmentation, and ablation details in Appendices~\ref{app:failure-modes}--\ref{app:ablations}.
121
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \\emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\%), while $10{\\times}$ model scaling and Reflexion each reduce death rates to ${\\sim}$35\\%, yet none closes the ${\\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

```json
          " The Signal Concentration Index (SCI $= 0.2147$; Appendix~\\ref{app:sci}) confirms low concentration: the top-3 task types account for only $39.8\\%$ of total death-rate differential. One outlier, \\texttt{find-non-living-thing}, has zero deaths ($\\Delta = +9.58$ from non-death scores alone); all 10 remaining types exhibit death-asymmetry-driven routing labels.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Sensitivity analysis.}",
          "-Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types. Cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$, resampling task types) yields a wider $[73.3\\%, 100\\%]$; with $K < 40$ this should be interpreted cautiously~\\citep{cameron-2008}. A wild cluster bootstrap ($K{=}11$ task clusters, $B{=}9{,}999$) confirms significance at the cluster level ($p < 0.001$, Cohen's $d = 3.02$, 95\\% CI $[0.73, 1.00]$; Appendix~\\ref{app:wild-cluster}). Both intervals exclude 50\\%, confirming that death avoidance accounts for a majority of routing signal under resampling. Across all 28 death-prone tasks (included and excluded), internal DR exceeds deterministic DR in every case (100\\% directional consistency), with pooled $\\Delta = +37.3\\%$ (95\\% CI: $[29.7\\%, 44.4\\%]$); 15 of 28 are individually significant, with the 13 non-significant cases entirely explained by small sample size ($n{=}5$; Appendix~\\ref{app:per-task-ci}). An 8-seed cross-validation of the router ($47.1 \\pm 2.2\\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy, all seeds below chance) confirms that the failure to learn routing generalizes across data splits (Appendix~\\ref{app:seed-robustness}).",
          "+Episode-level bootstrap ($B = 10{,}000$) yields 95\\% CI $[81.6\\%, 98.8\\%]$ for DAF; per-task CIs confirm $\\Delta > 0$ for 10 of 11 types.     "originalFile": "\\begin{abstract}\nModular LLM agents that route between neural reasoning and deterministic computation promise improved reliability, yet no systematic methodology exists for diagnosing \\emph{what} drives routing decisions. We introduce an episode-level counterfactual framework that decomposes routing signal into death-avoidance and non-death components. Applied to ScienceWorld (11 task types, 245 episodes, Qwen2.5-7B-Instruct), \\textbf{78.9\\% of non-tie routing labels} arise from death asymmetry (DAF $= 90.4\\%$): the internal strategy's hallucination-induced failures trigger catastrophic agent death, while the deterministic strategy survives. Cross-environment contrasts confirm framework predictions: on MATH ($N{=}795$), no routing signal emerges without catastrophic failure ($p = 0.81$).\n\\end{abstract}\n",
```

```text
365	\section{Failure Mode Analysis}
366	\label{app:failure-modes}
367	
368	All 73 internal-strategy deaths (100\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \texttt{focus on} (${\sim}0.5\%$ of actions vs.\ $5.5\%$ for internal in death episodes), which explains its 91.8\% survival rate.
369	
370	\begin{table}[h]
371	\centering
372	\small
373	\begin{tabular}{@{}clr@{}}
374	\toprule
375	Cat. & Description & \% \\
376	\midrule
377	A & No task awareness (pure \texttt{focus on} spam) & 21.9 \\
378	B & Task-aware but no action grounding & 69.9 \\
379	D & Genuine attempt with execution failure & 2.7 \\
380	E & Initial score wasted by early errors & 5.5 \\
381	\bottomrule
382	\end{tabular}
383	\caption{Taxonomy of 73 internal-strategy death episodes. Category B dominates: agents correctly parse the task description but cannot translate understanding into valid ScienceWorld action primitives. 78.1\% of dying agents (B+D+E) demonstrate task comprehension; 97.3\% (A+B) fail to ground any task-relevant action.}
384	\label{tab:death-taxonomy}
385	\end{table}
386	
387	\paragraph{Representative examples.}
388	\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \texttt{focus on kitchen}, \texttt{focus on door}, \texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \texttt{focus on} call. \emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.
389	
390	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
391	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy. Both episode types are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions ($87\%$ in death, $79\%$ in non-death), but \texttt{focus on} usage is $3{\times}$ higher in death episodes ($5.5\%$ vs.\ $1.8\%$). Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} accounts for 44.6\%, suggesting a systematic bias toward specific objects rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps. Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} contrasts the internal and deterministic strategies: the deterministic strategy exhibits a qualitatively different action profile with near-zero \texttt{focus on} usage, confirming that the grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.
392	
393	\begin{figure}[h]
394	\centering
395	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_distribution.pdf}
396	\caption{Action distribution for the internal (LLM) strategy in death ($n{=}74$) vs.\ non-death ($n{=}59$) episodes. Left: full distribution (both dominated by \texttt{connect}). Right: non-\texttt{connect} actions zoomed. \texttt{focus on} is $3{\times}$ more frequent in death episodes, while non-death episodes show higher rates of observation and object interaction.}
397	\label{fig:death-action-dist}
398	\end{figure}
399	
400	\begin{figure}[h]
401	\centering
402	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_timeline.pdf}
403	\caption{Step-by-step action sequences for 6 representative death episodes. The fatal \texttt{focus on} action (marked with arrow) consistently appears in the final steps after long \texttt{connect} sequences, illustrating the ``connect loop $\to$ fatal focus'' pattern. Examples are drawn from the full 30-task dataset to maximize task-type diversity; the pattern is identical in the 11-task analysis subset.}
404	\label{fig:death-timeline}
405	\end{figure}
406	
407	\begin{figure}[h]
408	\centering
409	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{figures/death_action_comparison.pdf}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Ty1i45ivLzR5srQ1X3TpPW`</summary>

```text
30	\begin{enumerate}
31	    \item \textbf{An episode-level counterfactual diagnostic framework} for decomposing routing signal in interactive environments, with dual penalty-independent and penalty-dependent metrics (Algorithm~\ref{alg:labeling}) and router training as a diagnostic probe of label informativeness. The framework requires only paired episode outcomes under two modalities with binary catastrophic indicators (\S\ref{sec:collection}--\ref{sec:daf}, \S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}).
32	
33	    \item \textbf{Evidence that death dominance is robust and routing value is near-degenerate}: 78.9\% of non-tie routing labels arise from death asymmetry, with DAF $= 90.4\%$ at $P{=}100$. The oracle router degenerates to unconditional deterministic computation (96.3\% decision overlap). Retry, $10{\times}$ model scaling, and Reflexion each fail to close the ${\sim}$18~pp gap to deterministic execution. A failure mode taxonomy identifies the root mechanism: 100\% of internal-strategy deaths stem from a single action-space grounding failure (\S\ref{sec:death-decomp}--\ref{sec:agent-improvement}, Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}).
34	
35	    \item \textbf{Cross-environment boundary probes}: MATH ($N{=}795$) confirms the predicted null ($p = 0.81$); ALFWorld ($N{=}135$) identifies information asymmetry as an alternative mechanism; a Llama pilot ($N{=}70$) shows cross-model directional consistency. These are boundary probes of framework applicability, not direct replications (\S\ref{sec:math-contrast}--\ref{sec:cross-model}).
36	\end{enumerate}
37	
38	
39
```

```json
  "old_string": "All 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} alone accounts for 44.6\\%, indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.",
  "new_string": "All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. Only 21.9\\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling."
```

```json
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 73 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} alone accounts for 44.6\\%, indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",
```
