          " \\label{app:failure-modes}",
          " ",
          "-All 73 internal-strategy deaths (100\\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action (equivalent to ``this is my final answer''); the LLM agent systematically misuses it as a general interaction or exploration command, executing it on irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items). Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes), which explains its 91.8\\% survival rate.",
          "+All 105 internal-strategy deaths (100\\%) are triggered by the same action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. Each such execution submits an incorrect answer, resulting in the $-100$ death penalty. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes), which explains its 91.8\\% survival rate.",

        "lines": [
          " \\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \\texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \\texttt{focus on kitchen}, \\texttt{focus on door}, \\texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \\texttt{focus on} call. \\emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \\texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \\texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}",
          "-Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy. Both episode types are dominated by \\texttt{connect} actions ($87\\%$ in death, $79\\%$ in non-death), but \\texttt{focus on} usage is $3{\\times}$ higher in death episodes ($5.5\\%$ vs.\\ $1.8\\%$). Among fatal \\texttt{focus on} targets, \\texttt{workshop} accounts for 44.6\\%, suggesting a systematic bias toward specific objects rather than random exploration. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \\texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \\texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison} contrasts the internal and deterministic strategies: the deterministic strategy exhibits a qualitatively different action profile with near-zero \\texttt{focus on} usage, confirming that the grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.",
          "+Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy. Among fatal \\texttt{focus on} targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic bias toward specific objects rather than random exploration. ```text
10	\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}
11	All 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \texttt{focus on} (${\sim}0.5\%$ of actions vs.\ $5.5\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\% survival (Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.
```

389	
390	\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}
391	Figure~\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy. Both episode types are dominated by \texttt{connect} actions ($87\%$ in death, $79\%$ in non-death), but \texttt{focus on} usage is $3{\times}$ higher in death episodes ($5.5\%$ vs.\ $1.8\%$). Among fatal \texttt{focus on} targets, \texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\%), followed by \texttt{inventory} (17.1\%) and \texttt{kitchen} (10.5\%), indicating a systematic bias toward specific objects rather than random exploration. Figure~\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps. Figure~\ref{fig:death-comparison} contrasts the internal and deterministic strategies: the deterministic strategy exhibits a qualitatively different action profile with near-zero \texttt{focus on} usage, confirming that the grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.
392	
```

        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " \\emph{Category A} (no task awareness): In a \\texttt{chemistry-mix} episode, the agent immediately executes \\texttt{focus on kitchen}, \\texttt{focus on door}, \\texttt{focus on inventory} without reading the task, dying on the first \\texttt{focus on} call. \\emph{Category B} (comprehension without grounding): In a \\texttt{lifespan-longest-lived} episode, the agent correctly identifies that it must compare animal lifespans and reasons about which animal lives longest, but then executes \\texttt{focus on oak tree} (a non-animal object in the room) instead of navigating to find the target animal, triggering death.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{Action distribution analysis.}",
          "-Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy. Both episode types are dominated by \\texttt{connect} actions ($87\\%$ in death, $79\\%$ in non-death), but \\texttt{focus on} usage is $3{\\times}$ higher in death episodes ($5.5\\%$ vs.\\ $1.8\\%$). Among fatal \\texttt{focus on} targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic bias toward specific objects rather than random exploration. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-timeline} visualizes the temporal pattern: agents execute long sequences of \\texttt{connect} actions before a terminal \\texttt{focus on} triggers death, typically in the final 1--3 steps. Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison} contrasts the internal and deterministic strategies: the deterministic strategy exhibits a qualitatively different action profile with near-zero \\texttt{focus on} usage, confirming that the grounding failure is specific to LLM-generated action selection.",
          "+Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist} shows the action distribution in death versus non-death episodes for the internal strategy.     "oldString": "A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes)",
    "newString": "A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) identifies action-space grounding failure as the root cause: the majority of dying agents correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives. The dominant failure mode is not comprehension failure but inability to translate task understanding into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.4\\%$ for internal in death episodes)",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Discussion}\n\\label{sec:discussion}\n\n\\paragraph{Why the router fails: task-type-level signal.}\nThe router diagnostic (\\S\\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reveals a structural mismatch: death avoidance is a task-type-level binary property (10 of 11 types carry death risk), but step-level features correlate weakly with this signal because death risk depends on multi-step state trajectories, not individual action semantics. The 4B router's episode-level improvement ($59.0\\%$) without step-level gains ($56$--$59\\%$, flat) confirms that capacity scaling captures task-type classification rather than generalizable routing features. A per-class confusion matrix of the 1.7B router reveals that 33\\% of steps in death episodes are mispredicted as ``internal'' (routing to the LLM in precisely the situations where it dies), with internal-class precision of only 0.14. The router's prediction rate is similar across death and non-death episodes, confirming that it cannot detect death risk from step-level features.\n\n\\paragraph{Non-triviality of death dominance.}\nThree qualitatively different interventions all fail to close the gap to deterministic-level safety (16.3\\%). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\\% vs.\\ baseline 42.9\\%, ${\\sim}$2~pp across three trials; Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). $10{\\times}$ capability scaling reduces death rate to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). Self-correction via Reflexion achieves a comparable reduction on death-prone tasks (${\\sim}$35\\%, $\\Delta \\approx -17$~pp). That two independent mechanisms (parameter scaling and verbal self-correction) converge to nearly identical death-rate floors suggests a shared bound set by the internal strategy's lack of ground-truth state access, not by any single intervention's limitations. Death dominance is robust under all three tested interventions. \\texttt{find-non-living-thing} exhibits 0\\% death across all conditions, confirming that death risk is task-specific. Compounding state estimation errors across multi-step trajectories create catastrophic failures that single-axis improvements attenuate but do not eliminate. Tree search with environment feedback and frontier models remain untested. Prior tool-routing work \\citep{toolformer-2023,art-2023,chameleon-2023} operates in non-interactive settings where individual tool calls are independent; in multi-step interactive environments, the finding is not that death exists but that the death-rate differential ($\\delta = 26.6$~pp) and its downstream effects resist all three tested intervention axes.\n\n\\paragraph{Anatomy of catastrophic failure.}\nAll 105 internal-strategy deaths trace to a single action pattern: \\texttt{focus on [object]}. In ScienceWorld, \\texttt{focus on} is the answer-submission action; the LLM systematically misuses it as a general interaction command, applying it to irrelevant targets (rooms, doors, inventory items) and thereby submitting incorrect answers that trigger the $-100$ penalty. A taxonomy of death episodes (Appendix~\\ref{app:failure-modes}) reveals that 69.9\\% involve agents that correctly parse the task description but cannot ground their understanding in valid action primitives (Category B: comprehension without grounding). Only 21.9\\% show no task awareness (Category A). The root cause is thus action-space grounding failure, not comprehension failure: 78.1\\% of dying agents demonstrate task understanding, yet 97.3\\% fail to translate it into correct actions. The deterministic strategy rarely executes \\texttt{focus on} (${\\sim}0.5\\%$ of actions vs.\\ $5.5\\%$ for internal in death episodes), achieving 91.8\\% survival (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-comparison}). Among fatal targets, \\texttt{workshop} remains the most frequent (20.0\\%), followed by \\texttt{inventory} (17.1\\%) and \\texttt{kitchen} (10.5\\%), indicating a systematic object bias rather than random exploration (Figure~\\ref{fig:death-action-dist}). This specificity suggests that targeted interventions (action-space filtering, grounding-aware prompting) could reduce death rates more effectively than general capability scaling.\n\n\\paragraph{Oracle degeneracy and routing value.}\nThe oracle router matches always-deterministic on 96.3\\% of decisions, improving score by only 2.89 points ($-6.58$ vs.\\ $-9.47$). This \\emph{oracle degeneracy} challenges a core assumption of routing research: that learning to switch between modalities yields substantial gains over any fixed strategy. When death dominance holds, the optimal policy is near-degenerate because the dominant signal (avoiding catastrophic failure) uniformly favors deterministic computation. Practitioners should first verify that the oracle margin justifies the complexity of a learned router before investing in routing infrastructure.\n\n\\paragraph{Scaling predictions.}\nStronger backbones lower death rates but do not eliminate death dominance: Qwen2.5-72B reduces aggregate death from ${\\sim}$51\\% to ${\\sim}$35\\% ($-17$~pp), yet DAF remains $90.9\\%$ (vs.\\ $90.4\\%$ at 7B), and the gap to deterministic (16.3\\%) remains ${\\sim}$18~pp. The Llama pilot ($\\delta = +34.3\\%$) versus Qwen-7B ($\\delta = +26.6\\%$) is consistent in the capability-increase direction. Preliminary evidence (Qwen2.5-0.5B, $N{=}15$) suggests the relationship is non-monotonic: very weak models exhibit low death rates from inability to reach dangerous states. Our results suggest that in environments where one modality triggers irreversible catastrophic failures with severe penalties, death avoidance will dominate routing signal; self-correction mechanisms can selectively reduce death-driven signal but are unlikely to eliminate death dominance.\n\n\\paragraph{When to use deterministic computation.}\nOur framework provides a practical diagnostic sequence for practitioners deploying modular agents. First, identify whether the target environment contains catastrophic failure mechanisms (permadeath, irreversible penalties). Second, estimate the death-rate asymmetry between internal and deterministic strategies using a small pilot (${\\sim}$50 episodes suffice for directional signal). Third, if death-asymmetry labels dominate the routing signal, a fixed deterministic strategy is likely to match or exceed a learned router at substantially lower deployment complexity. The MATH negative control ($p = 0.81$) confirms that this diagnostic correctly identifies environments where routing adds no value.\n\n\\paragraph{DAF and framework applicability.}\nDAF increases monotonically with $P$ (Appendix~\\ref{app:penalty}); the penalty-independent label fraction (78.9\\%) is the more informative primary diagnostic. The framework requires paired episodes under two modalities, binary catastrophic outcomes, and sparse rewards. ScienceWorld satisfies all three; MATH lacks catastrophic outcomes (negative control); ALFWorld lacks them too, and the framework identifies information asymmetry as the alternative mechanism. When $P$ is small relative to $\\Delta_{\\text{nd}}$, DAF degrades (at $P{=}0$, DAF $= 7.5\\%$); the framework is most informative when catastrophic penalties dominate non-death score differences. NetHack~\\citep{nethack-2020} and Crafter~\\citep{crafter-2022}, both featuring permadeath, are the most direct replication targets; recent LLM agent benchmarks in these environments \\citep{balrog-2025,netplay-2024} provide the infrastructure for such evaluation.\n",

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MCaSYU2hZKy1BPX8XeN34w`</summary>

```text
302	\end{table}
303	
304	All scales hover at ${\sim}50\%$ episode-level balanced accuracy (equivalent to the always-deterministic baseline), with no monotonic improvement as data increases. Augmentation-based scaling provides no improvement at any scale; however, episode-level minority-class diversity remains a potential confound that cannot be resolved without collecting more internal-preferred episodes. The ground-truth internal-preferred rate (9/245 $= 3.7\%$) means collecting 50 internal-preferred episodes would require approximately 1{,}350 paired episodes at current environment dynamics, a $5.5{\times}$ expansion with diminishing return on a $9.6\%$ signal. The failure points instead to a structural limitation: when DAF $= 90.4\%$ and all task types are deterministic-majority (86:9 label ratio), the episode-level routing problem is trivially solved by always choosing deterministic, leaving no learnable signal for a step-level router to exploit.
305	
306	Augmentation disproportionately increases the majority (deterministic) class: Det\% rises from 89.6\% at 100\% to 94.2\% at 200\% and ${\sim}97.7\%$ at 800\%. However, the complete collapse to majority-class prediction (internal recall $= 0.0$) occurs even at the smallest unaugmented scale (25\%, ${\sim}90\%$ det); this is not an augmentation artifact. Balanced accuracy corrects for class imbalance by averaging per-class recall, so the metric itself is not biased by the skew.
```

### Tool result

_source line 1128 · 2026-05-18T09:43:29.078Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01Pa7TZYAF2CvHgGvBP1A7Yw`</summary>

```text
50	\end{table}
51	
52	We test whether agent-level improvement reduces death dominance. Retry gives the agent three attempts per episode without self-correction; Reflexion~\citep{reflexion-2023} adds verbal self-reflection between trials (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}). Retry yields only marginal reduction (40.9\% vs.\ 42.9\% baseline; the 2.0~pp difference is within sampling noise, Fisher exact $p > 0.6$). Reflexion reduces death to 31.4\% by rescuing 29.3\% of first-trial deaths (27 of 92), but the gap to deterministic (16.3\%) remains 15.1~pp. Trial~1 death rate under Reflexion (41.8\%) matches the single-trial baseline; the reduction comes from cross-trial self-correction, not from the Reflexion prompt itself. Trial~3 adds minimal rescue beyond Trial~2 (net $+4$). Scaling from 7B to Qwen2.5-72B ($10{\times}$ parameters) moderately reduces aggregate death rate from ${\sim}$51\% to ${\sim}$35\% ($\Delta \approx -17$~pp) across death-prone tasks (Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement}), but does not approach deterministic-level safety (16.3\%). Task-level variation ranges from $+5$~pp (chemistry-mix, where 72B performs slightly worse) to $-31$~pp (lifespan-longest-lived). This variation suggests that scaling yields large reductions in biological-reasoning tasks ($-15$ to $-31$~pp) but provides no benefit for chemical state tracking (chemistry-mix: $+5$~pp; Appendix~\ref{app:cross-model}). The 31.4\% in Table~\ref{tab:agent-improvement} covers all 11 tasks; on the 10 death-prone tasks, Reflexion achieves ${\sim}$35\%, the figure reported in the Abstract. Recomputing DAF with 72B as the internal strategy ($N{=}220$) yields $90.9\%$ (95\% CI: $[83.9\%, 97.3\%]$), nearly identical to the 7B value ($90.4\%$); the death-asymmetry label fraction rises from $78.9\%$ (7B) to $84.3\%$ (72B). On death-prone tasks ($N{=}200$), DAF reaches $99.5\%$ ($[98.0\%, 100.2\%]$) with $95.9\%$ of labels driven by death asymmetry. A matched 10-task comparison confirms scale-independence: 8 of 10 death-prone tasks maintain DAF $= 100\%$ at both 7B and 72B; episode-weighted DAF declines only from $100\%$ to $97.5\%$. No task shifts from death-dominated to performance-dominated at $10{\times}$ scale. Scaling lowers death rate but leaves death dominance structurally intact.
53	
54	\subsection{Cross-Domain Negative Contrast: MATH}
```

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01SrTzvg5n1hhbJYv6CYVHiw`</summary>

```text
207	With gradient accumulation of 1 (effective batch size 4), routing performance is highly sensitive to the evaluation split; this suggests overfitting to specific train/validation partition boundaries. With gradient accumulation of 8 (effective batch size 32), step-level performance stabilizes across splits (${\sim}54\%$ balanced accuracy). All reported results use gradient accumulation 8.
208	
209	Under step-level evaluation with a fixed data split, cross-seed balanced accuracy ranges from 52.7\% to 58.1\%, but internal recall varies from 51.5\% to 81.8\%, which reflects threshold sensitivity. However, episode-level evaluation reveals that this apparent discrimination does not generalize: balanced accuracy drops to $47.1 \pm 2.2\%$ across 8 cross-split seeds (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}). Deployed end-to-end, the trained router allocates 97.0\% of episodes to deterministic computation with 17.6\% death rate, and misrouting cost (death from internally-routed episodes) exceeds the benefit of correctly routing rare internal-preferred cases.
210	
211	\paragraph{Evaluation methods.} All results use \emph{generation-based evaluation}: the model generates a routing label as text (\texttt{enable\_thinking=False}), parsed for classification. We evaluate at two granularities: \emph{step-level} (steps from the same episode may appear in both train and validation sets) and \emph{episode-level} (entire episodes held out). Step-level accuracy ($54.1 \pm 5.4\%$) exceeds chance (the router captures some signal), but episode-level accuracy ($47.1 \pm 2.2\%$, below chance) shows this does not generalize to unseen episodes. Augmentation-based data scaling (Appendix~\ref{app:seed-robustness}) provides no improvement; real-data scaling remains open. Always-deterministic is trivially near-optimal given DAF $= 90.4\%$. The main text (\S\ref{sec:router-diagnostic}) reports controlled 4-seed results at three scales; the model-size ablation (Appendix~\ref{app:ablations}) provides further detail.
```
